I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really kept track of it in an organized way or anything. I mean, I do think a lot about what I’m eating, probably more than I should, but I don’t know if I’d say I have a clear rule like, “I’m not allowed to eat this,” or “I can only eat that.” Some days I’ll tell myself I should be “good” or eat lighter, especially after I’ve had one of those episodes, but then other days it sort of falls apart.

So… yes, maybe in a loose way, but I’m not sure if it counts as restricting the way you mean. It’s more like I get anxious and try to compensate, and then I end up feeling pulled back and forth around food.

I’m not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds strange, because you’d think I’d know exactly, given how much I worry about it, but I don’t have a clear number to give you.

I don’t weigh myself in a very consistent way—sometimes I avoid the scale because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I check and then wish I hadn’t. So I can’t honestly say, “yes, I’ve lost this much” or “gained this much.” My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not in a way I can trust, because some days I feel bigger no matter what I’m wearing.

So… I haven’t noticed a major, obvious change, but I’m also not sure I’m the best judge of it right now.

It’s gotten past being some one-time mistake, and that’s what really frightened me. I feel ashamed, and I don’t really know how to get a handle on it by myself.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s most weeks now. I hate saying that, because part of me still wants to make it sound like it’s just every now and then, like I just slipped up once in a while. But if I’m being honest, for the past several months, there have been these times where I eat in secret and then afterward I feel panicked and ashamed, and I go and make myself sick.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I can honestly call it occasional anymore. It’s become enough of a pattern that I know it’s happening most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict the amount or types of food you eat between these episodes of bingeing and purging?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really kept track of it in an organized way or anything. I mean, I do think a lot about what I’m eating, probably more than I should, but I don’t know if I’d say I have a clear rule like, “I’m not allowed to eat this,” or “I can only eat that.” Some days I’ll tell myself I should be “good” or eat lighter, especially after I’ve had one of those episodes, but then other days it sort of falls apart.\n\nSo… yes, maybe in a loose way, but I’m not sure if it counts as restricting the way you mean. It’s more like I get anxious and try to compensate, and then I end up feeling pulled back and forth around food.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds strange, because you’d think I’d know exactly, given how much I worry about it, but I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI don’t weigh myself in a very consistent way—sometimes I avoid the scale because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I check and then wish I hadn’t. So I can’t honestly say, “yes, I’ve lost this much” or “gained this much.” My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not in a way I can trust, because some days I feel bigger no matter what I’m wearing.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed a major, obvious change, but I’m also not sure I’m the best judge of it right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that at my age, because it sounds vain or silly, but it’s there all the time in the back of my mind.

It can be something small, like if I’m getting dressed for church and my skirt feels tight at the waist or around my hips, I’ll just feel this awful sinking feeling. Then I start fussing with everything, trying on different things, looking in the mirror from the side, and suddenly I’m convinced I look bigger than I should. Or at work, if I catch my reflection in one of the store mirrors, especially from the side, it can just ruin my whole day. I’ll think, “Everyone can tell. Everyone can see I’ve let myself go.”

And I know people probably aren’t studying me like that, but in the moment it feels so real. I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. So yes, gaining weight scares me, but it’s not just the number. It’s how I think I look, and what I imagine other people are seeing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… there have been times like that. Not always followed by making myself throw up, if that’s what you mean.

It’s usually not like I sit down planning it. It’s more like if I’ve been out late, like after bridge night, or if I get home from a late shift and I’m tired and keyed up and the house is quiet, I’ll start with something that seems normal. Maybe I’ll warm up some leftover casserole, just a small dish, and then while it’s heating I’ll have a few crackers. Then I remember there are cookies I baked, and I’ll tell myself I’m just having one or two, and then suddenly I’ve eaten several. And then I might have ice cream too, standing there or sitting at the kitchen table, not even really enjoying it by the end.

And it’s definitely more than I would ever put on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the amount all together. It doesn’t feel like a regular meal or a snack. It feels like I’ve sort of crossed a line, but sometimes I don’t make myself throw up afterward. I just sit with that awful full feeling and regret it.

control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I start during those times, it feels like I’m on automatic, thinking I should stop before my husband notices but still reaching for one more thing.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating and inappropriate compensatory behaviors both occur, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For at least the past several months, it’s been happening most weeks that I have one of those secret eating spells and then go make myself sick afterward. I can’t honestly call it occasional anymore.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still showing up for work, church, and bridge, but I’ve started planning my day around avoiding certain foods or getting home alone afterward. It makes social things feel tense instead of enjoyable.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since my husband stopped working outside the home and my routines stopped feeling private.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S048\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds strange, because you’d think I’d know exactly, given how much I worry about it, but I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI don’t weigh myself in a very consistent way—sometimes I avoid the scale because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I check and then wish I hadn’t. So I can’t honestly say, “yes, I’ve lost this much” or “gained this much.” My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not in a way I can trust, because some days I feel bigger no matter what I’m wearing.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed a major, obvious change, but I’m also not sure I’m the best judge of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that at my age, because it sounds vain or silly, but it’s there all the time in the back of my mind.\n\nIt can be something small, like if I’m getting dressed for church and my skirt feels tight at the waist or around my hips, I’ll just feel this awful sinking feeling. Then I start fussing with everything, trying on different things, looking in the mirror from the side, and suddenly I’m convinced I look bigger than I should. Or at work, if I catch my reflection in one of the store mirrors, especially from the side, it can just ruin my whole day. I’ll think, “Everyone can tell. Everyone can see I’ve let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know people probably aren’t studying me like that, but in the moment it feels so real. I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. So yes, gaining weight scares me, but it’s not just the number. It’s how I think I look, and what I imagine other people are seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without later making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been times like that. Not always followed by making myself throw up, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s usually not like I sit down planning it. It’s more like if I’ve been out late, like after bridge night, or if I get home from a late shift and I’m tired and keyed up and the house is quiet, I’ll start with something that seems normal. Maybe I’ll warm up some leftover casserole, just a small dish, and then while it’s heating I’ll have a few crackers. Then I remember there are cookies I baked, and I’ll tell myself I’m just having one or two, and then suddenly I’ve eaten several. And then I might have ice cream too, standing there or sitting at the kitchen table, not even really enjoying it by the end.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would ever put on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the amount all together. It doesn’t feel like a regular meal or a snack. It feels like I’ve sort of crossed a line, but sometimes I don’t make myself throw up afterward. I just sit with that awful full feeling and regret it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, I know people probably don’t see me as harshly as I see myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening.

Like before church, if I’m getting dressed and my skirt feels even a little tight, especially at the waist or hips, I can get completely stuck on it. I’ll keep pulling at it, turning sideways, trying to see if it shows, wondering if everyone will notice. And then I’m not just thinking, “This skirt is tight,” I’m thinking, “I look awful, I’ve let myself go, people can tell.”

The same thing happens at work sometimes. There are mirrors around the store, and if I catch myself from the side, it can ruin my whole day. I’ll see my shape and feel like it’s much bigger or worse than I expected, and then I assume other people are seeing that too. Even if nobody says anything, I feel exposed, like they must be noticing.

So yes, I suppose my mind makes it different. I don’t really trust what I see, but I still react to it like it’s the truth.

control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I start during those times, it feels like I’m on automatic, thinking I should stop before my husband notices but still reaching for one more thing.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating and inappropriate compensatory behaviors both occur, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For at least the past several months, it’s been happening most weeks that I have one of those secret eating spells and then go make myself sick afterward. I can’t honestly call it occasional anymore.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still showing up for work, church, and bridge, but I’ve started planning my day around avoiding certain foods or getting home alone afterward. It makes social things feel tense instead of enjoyable.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since my husband stopped working outside the home and my routines stopped feeling private.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S048\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that at my age, because it sounds vain or silly, but it’s there all the time in the back of my mind.\n\nIt can be something small, like if I’m getting dressed for church and my skirt feels tight at the waist or around my hips, I’ll just feel this awful sinking feeling. Then I start fussing with everything, trying on different things, looking in the mirror from the side, and suddenly I’m convinced I look bigger than I should. Or at work, if I catch my reflection in one of the store mirrors, especially from the side, it can just ruin my whole day. I’ll think, “Everyone can tell. Everyone can see I’ve let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know people probably aren’t studying me like that, but in the moment it feels so real. I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. It’s more like if I’ve been out late, like after bridge night, or if I get home from a late shift and I’m tired and keyed up and the house is quiet, I’ll start with something that seems normal. Maybe I’ll warm up some leftover casserole, just a small dish, and then while it’s heating I’ll have a few crackers. Then I remember there are cookies I baked, and I’ll tell myself I’m just having one or two, and then suddenly I’ve eaten several. And then I might have ice cream too, standing there or sitting at the kitchen table, not even really enjoying it by the end.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would ever put on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the amount all together. It doesn’t feel like a regular meal or a snack. It feels like I’ve sort of crossed a line, but sometimes I don’t make myself throw up afterward. I just sit with that awful full feeling and regret it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know people probably don’t see me as harshly as I see myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening.\n\nLike before church, if I’m getting dressed and my skirt feels even a little tight, especially at the waist or hips, I can get completely stuck on it. I’ll keep pulling at it, turning sideways, trying to see if it shows, wondering if everyone will notice. And then I’m not just thinking, “This skirt is tight,” I’m thinking, “I look awful, I’ve let myself go, people can tell.”\n\nThe same thing happens at work sometimes. There are mirrors around the store, and if I catch myself from the side, it can ruin my whole day. I’ll see my shape and feel like it’s much bigger or worse than I expected, and then I assume other people are seeing that too. Even if nobody says anything, I feel exposed, like they must be noticing.\n\nSo yes, I suppose my mind makes it different. I don’t really trust what I see, but I still react to it like it’s the truth.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you make yourself throw up, is it usually because you feel like you've eaten too much?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yes… that’s usually the reason. It’s after I feel like I’ve eaten too much, like I’ve gone past what I meant to do, and then I panic about what it’s going to do to my weight.

It makes social things feel tense instead of enjoyable.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since my husband stopped working outside the home and my routines stopped feeling private.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S048\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without later making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been times like that. Not always followed by making myself throw up, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s usually not like I sit down planning it. It’s more like if I’ve been out late, like after bridge night, or if I get home from a late shift and I’m tired and keyed up and the house is quiet, I’ll start with something that seems normal. Maybe I’ll warm up some leftover casserole, just a small dish, and then while it’s heating I’ll have a few crackers. Then I remember there are cookies I baked, and I’ll tell myself I’m just having one or two, and then suddenly I’ve eaten several. And then I might have ice cream too, standing there or sitting at the kitchen table, not even really enjoying it by the end.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would ever put on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the amount all together. It doesn’t feel like a regular meal or a snack. It feels like I’ve sort of crossed a line, but sometimes I don’t make myself throw up afterward. I just sit with that awful full feeling and regret it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know people probably don’t see me as harshly as I see myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening.\n\nLike before church, if I’m getting dressed and my skirt feels even a little tight, especially at the waist or hips, I can get completely stuck on it. I’ll keep pulling at it, turning sideways, trying to see if it shows, wondering if everyone will notice. And then I’m not just thinking, “This skirt is tight,” I’m thinking, “I look awful, I’ve let myself go, people can tell.”\n\nThe same thing happens at work sometimes. There are mirrors around the store, and if I catch myself from the side, it can ruin my whole day. I’ll see my shape and feel like it’s much bigger or worse than I expected, and then I assume other people are seeing that too. Even if nobody says anything, I feel exposed, like they must be noticing.\n\nSo yes, I suppose my mind makes it different. I don’t really trust what I see, but I still react to it like it’s the truth.\n\nDoctor: When you make yourself throw up, is it usually because you feel like you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s usually the reason. It’s after I feel like I’ve eaten too much, like I’ve gone past what I meant to do, and then I panic about what it’s going to do to my weight.\n\nThat’s actually what scared me enough to come in. I’d eat too much, and then I’d go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so I wouldn’t gain weight. At first I tried to tell myself it was just a terrible mistake, something I wouldn’t do again, but it’s gotten to where it isn’t just a one-time thing anymore. And I feel ashamed saying that out loud, but yes… it’s mostly because I feel too full and frightened that I’ll gain.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I suppose if I truly didn’t worry about gaining weight or how I looked, maybe I wouldn’t feel that same panic afterward. But honestly, that worry is so tied up in it that it’s hard for me to separate the two.

What happens is, after I eat too much, I start thinking right away, “I’ve ruined it, I’m going to gain, I can’t let this stay in me.” And then I end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up. That’s the part that finally scared me into coming here, because it isn’t feeling like some one-time awful mistake anymore. It’s become something I do because I’m afraid of gaining weight, and I hate admitting that, but yes… that’s the reason.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… well, I’m embarrassed to even say it out loud, but what finally scared me enough to make the appointment is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up. I tell myself it’s because I don’t want to gain weight, and at first I tried to convince myself it was just one bad choice, just something I did once and wouldn’t do again.\n\nBut it’s not feeling like that anymore. It’s gotten past being some one-time mistake, and that’s what really frightened me. I feel ashamed, and I don’t really know how to get a handle on it by myself.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s most weeks now. I hate saying that, because part of me still wants to make it sound like it’s just every now and then, like I just slipped up once in a while. But if I’m being honest, for the past several months, there have been these times where I eat in secret and then afterward I feel panicked and ashamed, and I go and make myself sick.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I can honestly call it occasional anymore. It’s become enough of a pattern that I know it’s happening most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict the amount or types of food you eat between these episodes of bingeing and purging?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really kept track of it in an organized way or anything. I mean, I do think a lot about what I’m eating, probably more than I should, but I don’t know if I’d say I have a clear rule like, “I’m not allowed to eat this,” or “I can only eat that.” Some days I’ll tell myself I should be “good” or eat lighter, especially after I’ve had one of those episodes, but then other days it sort of falls apart.\n\nSo… yes, maybe in a loose way, but I’m not sure if it counts as restricting the way you mean. It’s more like I get anxious and try to compensate, and then I end up feeling pulled back and forth around food.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds strange, because you’d think I’d know exactly, given how much I worry about it, but I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI don’t weigh myself in a very consistent way—sometimes I avoid the scale because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I check and then wish I hadn’t. So I can’t honestly say, “yes, I’ve lost this much” or “gained this much.” My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not in a way I can trust, because some days I feel bigger no matter what I’m wearing.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed a major, obvious change, but I’m also not sure I’m the best judge of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that at my age, because it sounds vain or silly, but it’s there all the time in the back of my mind.\n\nIt can be something small, like if I’m getting dressed for church and my skirt feels tight at the waist or around my hips, I’ll just feel this awful sinking feeling. Then I start fussing with everything, trying on different things, looking in the mirror from the side, and suddenly I’m convinced I look bigger than I should. Or at work, if I catch my reflection in one of the store mirrors, especially from the side, it can just ruin my whole day. I’ll think, “Everyone can tell. Everyone can see I’ve let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know people probably aren’t studying me like that, but in the moment it feels so real. I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. So yes, gaining weight scares me, but it’s not just the number. It’s how I think I look, and what I imagine other people are seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without later making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been times like that. Not always followed by making myself throw up, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s usually not like I sit down planning it. It’s more like if I’ve been out late, like after bridge night, or if I get home from a late shift and I’m tired and keyed up and the house is quiet, I’ll start with something that seems normal. Maybe I’ll warm up some leftover casserole, just a small dish, and then while it’s heating I’ll have a few crackers. Then I remember there are cookies I baked, and I’ll tell myself I’m just having one or two, and then suddenly I’ve eaten several. And then I might have ice cream too, standing there or sitting at the kitchen table, not even really enjoying it by the end.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would ever put on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the amount all together. It doesn’t feel like a regular meal or a snack. It feels like I’ve sort of crossed a line, but sometimes I don’t make myself throw up afterward. I just sit with that awful full feeling and regret it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know people probably don’t see me as harshly as I see myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening.\n\nLike before church, if I’m getting dressed and my skirt feels even a little tight, especially at the waist or hips, I can get completely stuck on it. I’ll keep pulling at it, turning sideways, trying to see if it shows, wondering if everyone will notice. And then I’m not just thinking, “This skirt is tight,” I’m thinking, “I look awful, I’ve let myself go, people can tell.”\n\nThe same thing happens at work sometimes. There are mirrors around the store, and if I catch myself from the side, it can ruin my whole day. I’ll see my shape and feel like it’s much bigger or worse than I expected, and then I assume other people are seeing that too. Even if nobody says anything, I feel exposed, like they must be noticing.\n\nSo yes, I suppose my mind makes it different. I don’t really trust what I see, but I still react to it like it’s the truth.\n\nDoctor: When you make yourself throw up, is it usually because you feel like you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s usually the reason. It’s after I feel like I’ve eaten too much, like I’ve gone past what I meant to do, and then I panic about what it’s going to do to my weight.\n\nThat’s actually what scared me enough to come in. I’d eat too much, and then I’d go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so I wouldn’t gain weight. At first I tried to tell myself it was just a terrible mistake, something I wouldn’t do again, but it’s gotten to where it isn’t just a one-time thing anymore. And I feel ashamed saying that out loud, but yes… it’s mostly because I feel too full and frightened that I’ll gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I suppose if I truly didn’t worry about gaining weight or how I looked, maybe I wouldn’t feel that same panic afterward. But honestly, that worry is so tied up in it that it’s hard for me to separate the two.\n\nWhat happens is, after I eat too much, I start thinking right away, “I’ve ruined it, I’m going to gain, I can’t let this stay in me.” And then I end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really. At least, not that I can think of.

When it happens, it’s after I’ve eaten too much and I get scared about gaining weight. That’s the part that finally frightened me enough to come in here, because I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so it won’t stay in me, so it won’t turn into weight. I wish I could say it was because I felt sick or something like that, but it isn’t really that.

It’s more like I feel I’ve made a mistake by eating too much, and then I panic and think I have to undo it. And it’s gotten past where I can tell myself, “Oh, that was just one awful time.” It’s happened enough now that I know it isn’t just a one-time mistake anymore. That’s what scares me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… well, I’m embarrassed to even say it out loud, but what finally scared me enough to make the appointment is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up. I tell myself it’s because I don’t want to gain weight, and at first I tried to convince myself it was just one bad choice, just something I did once and wouldn’t do again.\n\nBut it’s not feeling like that anymore. It’s gotten past being some one-time mistake, and that’s what really frightened me. I feel ashamed, and I don’t really know how to get a handle on it by myself.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s most weeks now. I hate saying that, because part of me still wants to make it sound like it’s just every now and then, like I just slipped up once in a while. But if I’m being honest, for the past several months, there have been these times where I eat in secret and then afterward I feel panicked and ashamed, and I go and make myself sick.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I can honestly call it occasional anymore. It’s become enough of a pattern that I know it’s happening most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict the amount or types of food you eat between these episodes of bingeing and purging?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really kept track of it in an organized way or anything. I mean, I do think a lot about what I’m eating, probably more than I should, but I don’t know if I’d say I have a clear rule like, “I’m not allowed to eat this,” or “I can only eat that.” Some days I’ll tell myself I should be “good” or eat lighter, especially after I’ve had one of those episodes, but then other days it sort of falls apart.\n\nSo… yes, maybe in a loose way, but I’m not sure if it counts as restricting the way you mean. It’s more like I get anxious and try to compensate, and then I end up feeling pulled back and forth around food.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds strange, because you’d think I’d know exactly, given how much I worry about it, but I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI don’t weigh myself in a very consistent way—sometimes I avoid the scale because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I check and then wish I hadn’t. So I can’t honestly say, “yes, I’ve lost this much” or “gained this much.” My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not in a way I can trust, because some days I feel bigger no matter what I’m wearing.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed a major, obvious change, but I’m also not sure I’m the best judge of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that at my age, because it sounds vain or silly, but it’s there all the time in the back of my mind.\n\nIt can be something small, like if I’m getting dressed for church and my skirt feels tight at the waist or around my hips, I’ll just feel this awful sinking feeling. Then I start fussing with everything, trying on different things, looking in the mirror from the side, and suddenly I’m convinced I look bigger than I should. Or at work, if I catch my reflection in one of the store mirrors, especially from the side, it can just ruin my whole day. I’ll think, “Everyone can tell. Everyone can see I’ve let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know people probably aren’t studying me like that, but in the moment it feels so real. I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. So yes, gaining weight scares me, but it’s not just the number. It’s how I think I look, and what I imagine other people are seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without later making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been times like that. Not always followed by making myself throw up, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s usually not like I sit down planning it. It’s more like if I’ve been out late, like after bridge night, or if I get home from a late shift and I’m tired and keyed up and the house is quiet, I’ll start with something that seems normal. Maybe I’ll warm up some leftover casserole, just a small dish, and then while it’s heating I’ll have a few crackers. Then I remember there are cookies I baked, and I’ll tell myself I’m just having one or two, and then suddenly I’ve eaten several. And then I might have ice cream too, standing there or sitting at the kitchen table, not even really enjoying it by the end.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would ever put on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the amount all together. It doesn’t feel like a regular meal or a snack. It feels like I’ve sort of crossed a line, but sometimes I don’t make myself throw up afterward. I just sit with that awful full feeling and regret it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know people probably don’t see me as harshly as I see myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening.\n\nLike before church, if I’m getting dressed and my skirt feels even a little tight, especially at the waist or hips, I can get completely stuck on it. I’ll keep pulling at it, turning sideways, trying to see if it shows, wondering if everyone will notice. And then I’m not just thinking, “This skirt is tight,” I’m thinking, “I look awful, I’ve let myself go, people can tell.”\n\nThe same thing happens at work sometimes. There are mirrors around the store, and if I catch myself from the side, it can ruin my whole day. I’ll see my shape and feel like it’s much bigger or worse than I expected, and then I assume other people are seeing that too. Then I start fussing with everything, trying on different things, looking in the mirror from the side, and suddenly I’m convinced I look bigger than I should. Or at work, if I catch my reflection in one of the store mirrors, especially from the side, it can just ruin my whole day. I’ll think, “Everyone can tell. Everyone can see I’ve let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know people probably aren’t studying me like that, but in the moment it feels so real. I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. So yes, gaining weight scares me, but it’s not just the number. It’s how I think I look, and what I imagine other people are seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without later making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been times like that. Not always followed by making myself throw up, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s usually not like I sit down planning it. It’s more like if I’ve been out late, like after bridge night, or if I get home from a late shift and I’m tired and keyed up and the house is quiet, I’ll start with something that seems normal. Maybe I’ll warm up some leftover casserole, just a small dish, and then while it’s heating I’ll have a few crackers. Then I remember there are cookies I baked, and I’ll tell myself I’m just having one or two, and then suddenly I’ve eaten several. And then I might have ice cream too, standing there or sitting at the kitchen table, not even really enjoying it by the end.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would ever put on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the amount all together. It doesn’t feel like a regular meal or a snack. It feels like I’ve sort of crossed a line, but sometimes I don’t make myself throw up afterward. I just sit with that awful full feeling and regret it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know people probably don’t see me as harshly as I see myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening.\n\nLike before church, if I’m getting dressed and my skirt feels even a little tight, especially at the waist or hips, I can get completely stuck on it. I’ll keep pulling at it, turning sideways, trying to see if it shows, wondering if everyone will notice. And then I’m not just thinking, “This skirt is tight,” I’m thinking, “I look awful, I’ve let myself go, people can tell.”\n\nThe same thing happens at work sometimes. There are mirrors around the store, and if I catch myself from the side, it can ruin my whole day. I’ll see my shape and feel like it’s much bigger or worse than I expected, and then I assume other people are seeing that too. Even if nobody says anything, I feel exposed, like they must be noticing.\n\nSo yes, I suppose my mind makes it different. I don’t really trust what I see, but I still react to it like it’s the truth.\n\nDoctor: When you make yourself throw up, is it usually because you feel like you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s usually the reason. It’s after I feel like I’ve eaten too much, like I’ve gone past what I meant to do, and then I panic about what it’s going to do to my weight.\n\nThat’s actually what scared me enough to come in. I’d eat too much, and then I’d go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so I wouldn’t gain weight. At first I tried to tell myself it was just a terrible mistake, something I wouldn’t do again, but it’s gotten to where it isn’t just a one-time thing anymore. And I feel ashamed saying that out loud, but yes… it’s mostly because I feel too full and frightened that I’ll gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I suppose if I truly didn’t worry about gaining weight or how I looked, maybe I wouldn’t feel that same panic afterward. But honestly, that worry is so tied up in it that it’s hard for me to separate the two.\n\nWhat happens is, after I eat too much, I start thinking right away, “I’ve ruined it, I’m going to gain, I can’t let this stay in me.” And then I end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up. That’s the part that finally scared me into coming here, because it isn’t feeling like some one-time awful mistake anymore. It’s become something I do because I’m afraid of gaining weight, and I hate admitting that, but yes… that’s the reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up for reasons other than trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No… not really. At least, not that I can think of.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s after I’ve eaten too much and I get scared about gaining weight. That’s the part that finally frightened me enough to come in here, because I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so it won’t stay in me, so it won’t turn into weight. I wish I could say it was because I felt sick or something like that, but it isn’t really that.\n\nIt’s more like I feel I’ve made a mistake by eating too much, and then I panic and think I have to undo it. And it’s gotten past where I can tell myself, “Oh, that was just one awful time.” It’s happened enough now that I know it isn’t just a one-time mistake anymore. That’s what scares me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I do. That’s one of the parts that feels so upsetting, because it doesn’t feel like a normal decision anymore once it starts.

I’ll tell myself, “Just stop now, put it away, your husband might notice,” and I really do think that in the moment. It’s not like I’m completely unaware. But then it’s like I’m on automatic, and my hand is still going for one more thing, and then one more after that. I’ll be standing there thinking I should stop before it gets obvious, before he wonders what I’m doing, and at the same time I’m still eating.

It’s hard to explain without sounding foolish, because of course I know I’m the one doing it. But it feels like I’ve lost the brakes. Like the sensible part of me is there, saying, “Enough, stop,” and another part just keeps going anyway.

I’m not completely sure, because the fear of gaining weight is so mixed in with it for me. But if I’m being honest, the large amount part can start before I’m even thinking clearly about that.

Like after bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired, I might start with just a little bit of leftover casserole, and then it turns into more casserole, and then I’ll see cookies I baked and have those, and then crackers, and then ice cream. All in one sitting. It’s not anything I would ever serve myself on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the actual amount all together.

So… I don’t know if I’d still do it without worrying about my weight. Maybe sometimes, yes, because it feels like once I start, I just keep going. But the worry afterward is a big part of what makes it feel so awful.

So yes, gaining weight scares me, but it’s not just the number. Not always followed by making myself throw up, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s usually not like I sit down planning it. It’s more like if I’ve been out late, like after bridge night, or if I get home from a late shift and I’m tired and keyed up and the house is quiet, I’ll start with something that seems normal. Maybe I’ll warm up some leftover casserole, just a small dish, and then while it’s heating I’ll have a few crackers. Then I remember there are cookies I baked, and I’ll tell myself I’m just having one or two, and then suddenly I’ve eaten several. And then I might have ice cream too, standing there or sitting at the kitchen table, not even really enjoying it by the end.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would ever put on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the amount all together. It doesn’t feel like a regular meal or a snack. It feels like I’ve sort of crossed a line, but sometimes I don’t make myself throw up afterward. I just sit with that awful full feeling and regret it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know people probably don’t see me as harshly as I see myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening.\n\nLike before church, if I’m getting dressed and my skirt feels even a little tight, especially at the waist or hips, I can get completely stuck on it. I’ll keep pulling at it, turning sideways, trying to see if it shows, wondering if everyone will notice. And then I’m not just thinking, “This skirt is tight,” I’m thinking, “I look awful, I’ve let myself go, people can tell.”\n\nThe same thing happens at work sometimes. There are mirrors around the store, and if I catch myself from the side, it can ruin my whole day. I’ll see my shape and feel like it’s much bigger or worse than I expected, and then I assume other people are seeing that too. Even if nobody says anything, I feel exposed, like they must be noticing.\n\nSo yes, I suppose my mind makes it different. I don’t really trust what I see, but I still react to it like it’s the truth.\n\nDoctor: When you make yourself throw up, is it usually because you feel like you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s usually the reason. It’s after I feel like I’ve eaten too much, like I’ve gone past what I meant to do, and then I panic about what it’s going to do to my weight.\n\nThat’s actually what scared me enough to come in. I’d eat too much, and then I’d go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so I wouldn’t gain weight. At first I tried to tell myself it was just a terrible mistake, something I wouldn’t do again, but it’s gotten to where it isn’t just a one-time thing anymore. And I feel ashamed saying that out loud, but yes… it’s mostly because I feel too full and frightened that I’ll gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I suppose if I truly didn’t worry about gaining weight or how I looked, maybe I wouldn’t feel that same panic afterward. But honestly, that worry is so tied up in it that it’s hard for me to separate the two.\n\nWhat happens is, after I eat too much, I start thinking right away, “I’ve ruined it, I’m going to gain, I can’t let this stay in me.” And then I end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up. That’s the part that finally scared me into coming here, because it isn’t feeling like some one-time awful mistake anymore. It’s become something I do because I’m afraid of gaining weight, and I hate admitting that, but yes… that’s the reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up for reasons other than trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No… not really. At least, not that I can think of.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s after I’ve eaten too much and I get scared about gaining weight. That’s the part that finally frightened me enough to come in here, because I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so it won’t stay in me, so it won’t turn into weight. I wish I could say it was because I felt sick or something like that, but it isn’t really that.\n\nIt’s more like I feel I’ve made a mistake by eating too much, and then I panic and think I have to undo it. And it’s gotten past where I can tell myself, “Oh, that was just one awful time.” It’s happened enough now that I know it isn’t just a one-time mistake anymore. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control when you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s one of the parts that feels so upsetting, because it doesn’t feel like a normal decision anymore once it starts.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just stop now, put it away, your husband might notice,” and I really do think that in the moment. It’s not like I’m completely unaware. But then it’s like I’m on automatic, and my hand is still going for one more thing, and then one more after that. I’ll be standing there thinking I should stop before it gets obvious, before he wonders what I’m doing, and at the same time I’m still eating.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding foolish, because of course I know I’m the one doing it. But it feels like I’ve lost the brakes. Like the sensible part of me is there, saying, “Enough, stop,” and another part just keeps going anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat large amounts of food in a short time if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, because the fear of gaining weight is so mixed in with it for me. But if I’m being honest, the large amount part can start before I’m even thinking clearly about that.\n\nLike after bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired, I might start with just a little bit of leftover casserole, and then it turns into more casserole, and then I’ll see cookies I baked and have those, and then crackers, and then ice cream. All in one sitting. It’s not anything I would ever serve myself on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the actual amount all together.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d still do it without worrying about my weight. Maybe sometimes, yes, because it feels like once I start, I just keep going. But the worry afterward is a big part of what makes it feel so awful.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

But the worry afterward is a big part of what makes it feel so awful.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're eating for pleasure or to satisfy hunger during these episodes, or is it more to cope with emotions?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, honestly. I don’t think it’s really pleasure in the way I’d normally mean pleasure, like sitting down and enjoying a nice meal. Sometimes the first few bites might feel comforting, or almost like a relief, but then it stops feeling like I’m enjoying it and starts feeling more frantic.

I don’t know that it’s hunger either, not most of the time. I might tell myself I’m hungry, or I might start because I haven’t eaten properly earlier, but once it gets going, it doesn’t feel like I’m calmly satisfying hunger. It’s more like I’m trying to quiet something down inside me, if that makes sense, though I couldn’t always tell you exactly what the feeling is. Sometimes I’m tired, or tense, or upset with myself, or just feeling sort of wound up, and food is there.

But I don’t always notice it in the moment. It’s usually afterward that I think, “What was that about?” During it, I’m not thinking very clearly. I’m just eating.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… well, I’m embarrassed to even say it out loud, but what finally scared me enough to make the appointment is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going into the bathroom and making myself throw up. I tell myself it’s because I don’t want to gain weight, and at first I tried to convince myself it was just one bad choice, just something I did once and wouldn’t do again.\n\nBut it’s not feeling like that anymore. It’s gotten past being some one-time mistake, and that’s what really frightened me. I feel ashamed, and I don’t really know how to get a handle on it by myself.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s most weeks now. I hate saying that, because part of me still wants to make it sound like it’s just every now and then, like I just slipped up once in a while. But if I’m being honest, for the past several months, there have been these times where I eat in secret and then afterward I feel panicked and ashamed, and I go and make myself sick.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I can honestly call it occasional anymore. It’s become enough of a pattern that I know it’s happening most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict the amount or types of food you eat between these episodes of bingeing and purging?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really kept track of it in an organized way or anything. I mean, I do think a lot about what I’m eating, probably more than I should, but I don’t know if I’d say I have a clear rule like, “I’m not allowed to eat this,” or “I can only eat that.” Some days I’ll tell myself I should be “good” or eat lighter, especially after I’ve had one of those episodes, but then other days it sort of falls apart.\n\nSo… yes, maybe in a loose way, but I’m not sure if it counts as restricting the way you mean. It’s more like I get anxious and try to compensate, and then I end up feeling pulled back and forth around food.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds strange, because you’d think I’d know exactly, given how much I worry about it, but I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI don’t weigh myself in a very consistent way—sometimes I avoid the scale because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I check and then wish I hadn’t. So I can’t honestly say, “yes, I’ve lost this much” or “gained this much.” My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not in a way I can trust, because some days I feel bigger no matter what I’m wearing.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed a major, obvious change, but I’m also not sure I’m the best judge of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that at my age, because it sounds vain or silly, but it’s there all the time in the back of my mind.\n\nIt can be something small, like if I’m getting dressed for church and my skirt feels tight at the waist or around my hips, I’ll just feel this awful sinking feeling. Then I start fussing with everything, trying on different things, looking in the mirror from the side, and suddenly I’m convinced I look bigger than I should. Or at work, if I catch my reflection in one of the store mirrors, especially from the side, it can just ruin my whole day. I’ll think, “Everyone can tell. Everyone can see I’ve let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know people probably aren’t studying me like that, but in the moment it feels so real. I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. So yes, gaining weight scares me, but it’s not just the number. It’s how I think I look, and what I imagine other people are seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without later making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been times like that. Not always followed by making myself throw up, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s usually not like I sit down planning it. It’s more like if I’ve been out late, like after bridge night, or if I get home from a late shift and I’m tired and keyed up and the house is quiet, I’ll start with something that seems normal. Maybe I’ll warm up some leftover casserole, just a small dish, and then while it’s heating I’ll have a few crackers. Then I remember there are cookies I baked, and I’ll tell myself I’m just having one or two, and then suddenly I’ve eaten several. And then I might have ice cream too, standing there or sitting at the kitchen table, not even really enjoying it by the end.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would ever put on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the amount all together. It doesn’t feel like a regular meal or a snack. It feels like I’ve sort of crossed a line, but sometimes I don’t make myself throw up afterward. I just sit with that awful full feeling and regret it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know people probably don’t see me as harshly as I see myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening.\n\nLike before church, if I’m getting dressed and my skirt feels even a little tight, especially at the waist or hips, I can get completely stuck on it. I’ll keep pulling at it, turning sideways, trying to see if it shows, wondering if everyone will notice. And then I’m not just thinking, “This skirt is tight,” I’m thinking, “I look awful, I’ve let myself go, people can tell.”\n\nThe same thing happens at work sometimes. There are mirrors around the store, and if I catch myself from the side, it can ruin my whole day. I’ll see my shape and feel like it’s much bigger or worse than I expected, and then I assume other people are seeing that too. Even if nobody says anything, I feel exposed, like they must be noticing.\n\nSo yes, I suppose my mind makes it different. I don’t really trust what I see, but I still react to it like it’s the truth.\n\nDoctor: When you make yourself throw up, is it usually because you feel like you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s usually the reason. It’s after I feel like I’ve eaten too much, like I’ve gone past what I meant to do, and then I panic about what it’s going to do to my weight.\n\nThat’s actually what scared me enough to come in. I’d eat too much, and then I’d go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so I wouldn’t gain weight. At first I tried to tell myself it was just a terrible mistake, something I wouldn’t do again, but it’s gotten to where it isn’t just a one-time thing anymore. And I feel ashamed saying that out loud, but yes… it’s mostly because I feel too full and frightened that I’ll gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I suppose if I truly didn’t worry about gaining weight or how I looked, maybe I wouldn’t feel that same panic afterward. But honestly, that worry is so tied up in it that it’s hard for me to separate the two.\n\nWhat happens is, after I eat too much, I start thinking right away, “I’ve ruined it, I’m going to gain, I can’t let this stay in me.” And then I end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up. That’s the part that finally scared me into coming here, because it isn’t feeling like some one-time awful mistake anymore. It’s become something I do because I’m afraid of gaining weight, and I hate admitting that, but yes… that’s the reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up for reasons other than trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No… not really. At least, not that I can think of.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s after I’ve eaten too much and I get scared about gaining weight. That’s the part that finally frightened me enough to come in here, because I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so it won’t stay in me, so it won’t turn into weight. I wish I could say it was because I felt sick or something like that, but it isn’t really that.\n\nIt’s more like I feel I’ve made a mistake by eating too much, and then I panic and think I have to undo it. And it’s gotten past where I can tell myself, “Oh, that was just one awful time.” It’s happened enough now that I know it isn’t just a one-time mistake anymore. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control when you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s one of the parts that feels so upsetting, because it doesn’t feel like a normal decision anymore once it starts.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just stop now, put it away, your husband might notice,” and I really do think that in the moment. It’s not like I’m completely unaware. But then it’s like I’m on automatic, and my hand is still going for one more thing, and then one more after that. I’ll be standing there thinking I should stop before it gets obvious, before he wonders what I’m doing, and at the same time I’m still eating.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding foolish, because of course I know I’m the one doing it. But it feels like I’ve lost the brakes. Like the sensible part of me is there, saying, “Enough, stop,” and another part just keeps going anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat large amounts of food in a short time if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, because the fear of gaining weight is so mixed in with it for me. But if I’m being honest, the large amount part can start before I’m even thinking clearly about that.\n\nLike after bridge night, or if I’ve worked a late shift and come home tired, I might start with just a little bit of leftover casserole, and then it turns into more casserole, and then I’ll see cookies I baked and have those, and then crackers, and then ice cream. All in one sitting. It’s not anything I would ever serve myself on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the actual amount all together.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d still do it without worrying about my weight. Maybe sometimes, yes, because it feels like once I start, I just keep going. But the worry afterward is a big part of what makes it feel so awful.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating for pleasure or to satisfy hunger during these episodes, or is it more to cope with emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, honestly. I don’t think it’s really pleasure in the way I’d normally mean pleasure, like sitting down and enjoying a nice meal. Sometimes the first few bites might feel comforting, or almost like a relief, but then it stops feeling like I’m enjoying it and starts feeling more frantic.\n\nI don’t know that it’s hunger either, not most of the time. I might tell myself I’m hungry, or I might start because I haven’t eaten properly earlier, but once it gets going, it doesn’t feel like I’m calmly satisfying hunger. It’s more like I’m trying to quiet something down inside me, if that makes sense, though I couldn’t always tell you exactly what the feeling is. Sometimes I’m tired, or tense, or upset with myself, or just feeling sort of wound up, and food is there.\n\nBut I don’t always notice it in the moment. It’s usually afterward that I think, “What was that about?” During it, I’m not thinking very clearly. I’m just eating.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not always, no… but more than I want to admit. That’s actually one of the things that finally scared me enough to come in here.

After I eat too much like that, I’ll start panicking about gaining weight, and then I go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. I hate even saying that out loud. At first I tried to tell myself it was just some awful one-time thing, like I’d lost my head and it wouldn’t happen again. But it hasn’t stayed that way. It’s happened enough now that I can’t pretend it was just a mistake or some strange accident.

I don’t do it after every single time, but when I’ve eaten a large amount, especially if I feel like I’ve really gone too far, the thought gets in my head that I have to undo it somehow. And then I’m in the bathroom, and afterward I feel ashamed and frightened by myself, because it feels like it’s becoming a pattern, not just a moment.

I tell myself it’s because I don’t want to gain weight, and at first I tried to convince myself it was just one bad choice, just something I did once and wouldn’t do again.\n\nBut it’s not feeling like that anymore. It’s gotten past being some one-time mistake, and that’s what really frightened me. I feel ashamed, and I don’t really know how to get a handle on it by myself.\n\nDoctor: How often have you been making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s most weeks now. I hate saying that, because part of me still wants to make it sound like it’s just every now and then, like I just slipped up once in a while. But if I’m being honest, for the past several months, there have been these times where I eat in secret and then afterward I feel panicked and ashamed, and I go and make myself sick.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I can honestly call it occasional anymore. It’s become enough of a pattern that I know it’s happening most weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict the amount or types of food you eat between these episodes of bingeing and purging?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really kept track of it in an organized way or anything. I mean, I do think a lot about what I’m eating, probably more than I should, but I don’t know if I’d say I have a clear rule like, “I’m not allowed to eat this,” or “I can only eat that.” Some days I’ll tell myself I should be “good” or eat lighter, especially after I’ve had one of those episodes, but then other days it sort of falls apart.\n\nSo… yes, maybe in a loose way, but I’m not sure if it counts as restricting the way you mean. It’s more like I get anxious and try to compensate, and then I end up feeling pulled back and forth around food.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds strange, because you’d think I’d know exactly, given how much I worry about it, but I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI don’t weigh myself in a very consistent way—sometimes I avoid the scale because I’m afraid of what it’ll say, and other times I check and then wish I hadn’t. So I can’t honestly say, “yes, I’ve lost this much” or “gained this much.” My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not in a way I can trust, because some days I feel bigger no matter what I’m wearing.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed a major, obvious change, but I’m also not sure I’m the best judge of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t like admitting that at my age, because it sounds vain or silly, but it’s there all the time in the back of my mind.\n\nIt can be something small, like if I’m getting dressed for church and my skirt feels tight at the waist or around my hips, I’ll just feel this awful sinking feeling. Then I start fussing with everything, trying on different things, looking in the mirror from the side, and suddenly I’m convinced I look bigger than I should. Or at work, if I catch my reflection in one of the store mirrors, especially from the side, it can just ruin my whole day. I’ll think, “Everyone can tell. Everyone can see I’ve let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know people probably aren’t studying me like that, but in the moment it feels so real. I feel embarrassed, almost exposed. So yes, gaining weight scares me, but it’s not just the number. It’s how I think I look, and what I imagine other people are seeing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short time without later making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… there have been times like that. Not always followed by making myself throw up, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s usually not like I sit down planning it. It’s more like if I’ve been out late, like after bridge night, or if I get home from a late shift and I’m tired and keyed up and the house is quiet, I’ll start with something that seems normal. Maybe I’ll warm up some leftover casserole, just a small dish, and then while it’s heating I’ll have a few crackers. Then I remember there are cookies I baked, and I’ll tell myself I’m just having one or two, and then suddenly I’ve eaten several. And then I might have ice cream too, standing there or sitting at the kitchen table, not even really enjoying it by the end.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would ever put on a plate in front of someone else. I’d be mortified if anyone saw the amount all together. It doesn’t feel like a regular meal or a snack. It feels like I’ve sort of crossed a line, but sometimes I don’t make myself throw up afterward. I just sit with that awful full feeling and regret it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know people probably don’t see me as harshly as I see myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when it’s happening.\n\nLike before church, if I’m getting dressed and my skirt feels even a little tight, especially at the waist or hips, I can get completely stuck on it. I’ll keep pulling at it, turning sideways, trying to see if it shows, wondering if everyone will notice. And then I’m not just thinking, “This skirt is tight,” I’m thinking, “I look awful, I’ve let myself go, people can tell.”\n\nThe same thing happens at work sometimes. There are mirrors around the store, and if I catch myself from the side, it can ruin my whole day. I’ll see my shape and feel like it’s much bigger or worse than I expected, and then I assume other people are seeing that too. Even if nobody says anything, I feel exposed, like they must be noticing.\n\nSo yes, I suppose my mind makes it different. I don’t really trust what I see, but I still react to it like it’s the truth.\n\nDoctor: When you make yourself throw up, is it usually because you feel like you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s usually the reason. It’s after I feel like I’ve eaten too much, like I’ve gone past what I meant to do, and then I panic about what it’s going to do to my weight.\n\nThat’s actually what scared me enough to come in. I’d eat too much, and then I’d go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so I wouldn’t gain weight. At first I tried to tell myself it was just a terrible mistake, something I wouldn’t do again, but it’s gotten to where it isn’t just a one-time thing anymore. And I feel ashamed saying that out loud, but yes… it’s mostly because I feel too full and frightened that I’ll gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I suppose if I truly didn’t worry about gaining weight or how I looked, maybe I wouldn’t feel that same panic afterward. But honestly, that worry is so tied up in it that it’s hard for me to separate the two.\n\nWhat happens is, after I eat too much, I start thinking right away, “I’ve ruined it, I’m going to gain, I can’t let this stay in me.” And then I end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up. It’s become something I do because I’m afraid of gaining weight, and I hate admitting that, but yes… that’s the reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up for reasons other than trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No… not really. At least, not that I can think of.\n\nWhen it happens, it’s after I’ve eaten too much and I get scared about gaining weight. That’s the part that finally frightened me enough to come in here, because I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself throw up so it won’t stay in me, so it won’t turn into weight. I wish I could say it was because I felt sick or something like that, but it isn’t really that.\n\nIt’s more like I feel I’ve made a mistake by eating too much, and then I panic and think I have to undo it.
